Corollary 42.6. Any connected compact Lie group is the quotient of
T × K by a finite central subgroup, where T = (S1)m is a torus and K
is semisimple and simply connected.

Proof. Let L be such a group, l its Lie algebra. It is reductive, so we can
uniquely decompose l as t ⊕k where t is the center and k is semisimple.
Let T, K ⊂L be the connected Lie subgroups corresponding to t, k. It is
clear that LieT = t, so T is closed, hence compact, hence a torus. Also
K is compact, so also closed, with LieK = k. Thus we have a surjective
homomorphism T × K →L whose kernel is finite, as desired.
□

43. Lecture 18

43.1. Polar decomposition. Now let us study the structure of the
connected Lie subgroup Gσ ⊂Gad corresponding to the real form gσ ⊂
g of a semisimple complex Lie algebra g. It is clear that this subgroup
is closed (LieGσ = gσ).
Let Kσ ⊂Gσ be the connected subgroup
corresponding to the Lie subalgebra kc ⊂gσ (the subgroup of elements
acting on g by unitary operators). The subgroup Kσ is closed since
Lie(K) = kc, hence it is compact. Also let Pσ = exp(ipc) ⊂Gσ (note
that Pσ is not a group, in general!). Since ipc acts on g by Hermitian
operators, the exponential map exp : ipc →Pσ is a diffeomorphism, so
Pσ ⊂Gσ is a closed embedded submanifold (the set of elements acting
on g by positive Hermitian operators).

Theorem 43.1. (Polar decomposition for Gσ) The multiplication map
µ : Kσ × Pσ →Gσ is a diffeomorphism. Thus Gσ ∼= Kσ × Rdim p as a
manifold (in particular, Gσ is homotopy equivalent to Kσ).

Proof. Recall that every invertible complex matrix A can be uniquely
written as a product A = UARA, where U = UA is a unitary ma-
trix and R = RA a positive Hermitian matrix, namely R = (A†A)1/2,
U = A(A†A)−1/2 (the classical polar decomposition). Let us consider
this decomposition for g ∈Gσ ⊂Aut(g) ⊂GL(g). Since g†g is an au-
tomorphism of g, so is (g†g)1/2 = Rg, so Rg ∈Pσ (a positive self-adjoint
element in Gσ). Also since Ug is unitary, it belongs to Kσ. Thus the
regular map g �→(Ug, Rg) is the inverse to µ (using the uniqueness of
the polar decomposition).
□
196


Corollary 43.2. The multiplication map defines a diffeomorphism

Gc
ad × P ∼= Gad,

where P is the set of elements acting on g by positive Hermitian oper-
ators. In particular, π1(Gad) = π1(Gc
ad) = P ∨/Q∨.

Corollary 43.3. If G is a semisimple complex Lie group then the cen-
ter Z of G is contained in Gc, i.e., coincides with the center Zc of Gc.
Thus the restriction of finite dimensional representations from G to Gc

is an equivalence of categories.

This also implies that by taking coverings the polar decomposition
applies verbatim to the real form Gσ ⊂G of any complex semisimple
Lie group G instead of Gad. We note, however, that if G is simply
connected, then Gσ need not be. In fact, its fundamental group could
be infinite. The simplest example is G = SL2(C), then for the split
form Gσ = SL2(R), which as we showed is homotopy equivalent to
SO(2) = S1, i.e. its fundamental group is Z.

Example 43.4. 1. For Gσ = SLn(C) we have Kσ = SU(n) and Pσ is
the set of positive Hermitian matrices of determinant 1, so the polar
decomposition in this case is the usual polar decomposition of complex
matrices.
2. For Gσ = SLn(R) we have Kσ = SO(n) and Pσ is the set of pos-
itive symmetric matrices of determinant 1, so the polar decomposition
in this case is the usual polar decomposition of real matrices.

43.2. Connected complex reductive groups.

Definition 43.5. A connected complex Lie group G is reductive if
it is of the form ((C×)r × G0)/Z where G0 is semisimple and Z is a
finite central subgroup. A complex Lie group G is reductive if G◦is
reductive and G/G◦is finite.

Example 43.6. GLn(C) = (C× × SLn(C))/µn is reductive.

It is clear that the Lie algebra LieG of any complex reductive Lie
group G is reductive, and any complex reductive Lie algebra is the Lie
algebra of a connected complex reductive Lie group. However, a simply
connected complex Lie group with a reductive Lie algebra need not be
reductive (e.g. G = C).
If G = ((C×)r × G0)/Z is a connected complex reductive Lie group
then as we have shown, Z ⊂(S1)r × Gc
0 ⊂(C×)r × G0, so we can
define the compact subgroup K ⊂G by K := ((S1)r × Gc
0)/Z. Then
it is easy to see that restriction of finite dimensional representations
from G to K is an equivalence, so representations of G are completely
197


reducible.
The irreducible representations are parametrized by col-
lections (n1, ..., nr, λ), λ ∈P+(G0), ni ∈Z, which define the trivial
character of Z.

43.3. Linear groups. A connected Lie group G (real or complex) is
called linear if it can be realized as a Lie subgroup of GLn(R), re-
spectively GLn(C). We have seen that any complex semisimple group
is linear. However, for real semisimple groups this is not so (e.g. the
universal cover of SL2(R) is not linear). In fact, we see that we can
characterize real semisimple groups as follows.

Proposition 43.7. Suppose gσ is a real form of a semisimple complex
Lie algebra g, G a complex Lie group with Lie algebra g, and Gσ its
Lie subgroup corresponding to the real Lie subalgebra gσ ⊂g. Then Gσ
is a linear group. Moreover, every connected real semisimple linear Lie
group has this form.

Exercise 43.8. Classify simply connected real semisimple linear Lie
groups.

43.4. Maximal tori in connected compact Lie groups. Let g be
a complex semisimple Lie algebra, gc its compact form, G a Lie group
with Lie algebra g, Gc ⊂G its compact part, as above.
A Cartan subalgebra hc ⊂gc is a maximal commutative Lie subal-
gebra (note that it automatically consists of semisimple elements since
all elements of gc are semisimple). In other words, it is a subspace such
that hc ⊗R C is a Cartan subalgebra of g.
Recall that all Cartan subalgebras of g are conjugate, even if equipped
with a system of simple roots. Namely, given two such subalgebras
(h, Π) and (h′, Π′), there is g ∈G such that g(h, Π)g−1 = (h′, Π′). The
same result holds for gc.

Lemma 43.9. Any two Cartan subalgebras in gc equipped with systems
of simple roots are conjugate under Gc.

Proof. Given (hc, Π) and (hc′, Π′), there is g ∈G such that g(hc, Π)g−1 =
(hc′, Π′). Then we also have g(hc, Π)g−1 = (hc′, Π′), where g := ω(g).
So g−1g commutes with hc, i.e., gh = g, where h ∈H. Writing g = kp,
where k ∈Gc, p ∈P, we have kp−1h = kp, so h = p2. Since p is
positive, p = h1/2, so it commutes with hc, thus k(hc, Π)k−1 = (hc′, Π′),
as claimed.
□

Note that for every Cartan subalgebra hc ⊂gc, Hc = exp(hc) ⊂Gc

is a torus, which is clearly maximal (as the Lie algebra of a larger
torus would be a larger commutative subalgebra than h). Conversely,
198


if Hc ⊂Gc is a maximal torus then Lie(Hc) can be included in a
Cartan subalgebra, hence it is itself a Cartan subalgebra. So we have
a bijection between Cartan subalgebras in gc and maximal tori in Gc.
Similarly, there is a bijection between Cartan subalgebras in g and
maximal tori in G.
This implies

Corollary 43.10. Any two maximal tori in G or Gc equipped with
systems of simple roots are conjugate.

Theorem 43.11. Every element of a connected compact Lie group K is
contained in a maximal torus, and all maximal tori in K are conjugate
(even when equipped with a system of simple roots).

Proof. We may assume without loss of generality that K is semisimple,
i.e., K = Gc for a semisimple complex Lie group G, which implies the
second statement. To prove the first statement, let K′ ⊂K be the set
of elements contained in a maximal torus. Fix a maximal torus T ⊂K
and consider the map f : K × T →K given by f(k, t) = ktk−1, whose
image is K′. This implies that K′ is compact, hence closed. On the
other hand, say that x ∈K is regular if its centralizer zx in gc has
dimension ≤r. This set is open and nonempty since there are plenty
of regular elements in gc. But every regular element is contained in the
maximal torus exp(zx), so the elements of K \ K′ are all non-regular.
But the set of non-regular elements is defined by polynomial equations
(minors of Ad(g) of codimension ≤r all vanish), so K \ K′ must be
empty.
□

Corollary 43.12. The exponential map exp : gc →Gc is surjective.

Exercise 43.13. Is the exponential map surjective for the group SL2(C)?

43.5. Semisimple and unipotent elements. Let G be a connected
reductive complex Lie group. An element g ∈G is called semisimple
if it acts in every finite dimensional representation of G by a semisimple
(=diagonalizable) operator, and unipotent if it acts in every finite di-
mensional representation of G by a unipotent operator (all eigenvalues
are 1).

G has a unique factorization g = gsgu, where gs ∈G is semisimple,

44. Lecture 19

44.1. Topology of Lie groups. We would now like to study topology
of connected Lie groups. As we know, any real semisimple Lie group
Gσ is diffeomorphic to the product of its compact subgroup Kσ and
a Euclidean space. This combined with Levi decomposition implies
that topology of connected Lie groups essentially reduces to topology
of compact ones.
So let us study cohomology of compact connected Lie groups.
201


We first recall some generalities on cohomology of manifolds. As we
mentioned before, the cohomology of an n-dimensional manifold M can
be computed by the de Rham complex

0 →Ω0(M) →Ω1(M) →... →Ωn(M) →0,

where Ωi(M) is the space of smooth (complex-valued) differential i-
forms on M. The maps in this complex are given by the differential
d : Ωi(M) →Ωi+1(M), which satisfies the equation d2 = 0. Namely,
we define the i-th de Rham cohomology of M as the quotient

Hi(M, C) := Ωi
closed(M)/Ωi
exact(M)

where Ωi
closed(M) ⊂Ωi(M) is the space of closed forms (such that
dω = 0) and Ωi
exact(M) ⊂Ωi(M) is the space of exact forms (such
that ω = dη for some η ∈Ωi−1(M)).
If M is compact then the spaces Hi(M, C) are known to be finite
dimensional, so we can define the Betti numbers of M, bi(M) :=
dim Hi(M, C). Note that b0(M) is the number of connected compo-
nents of M, so if M is connected then b0(M) = 1.
The wedge product of differential forms descends to the cohomology,
which makes H•(M, C) := ⊕n
i=0Hi(M, C) into a graded algebra. This
algebra is associative and graded-commutative: ab = (−1)deg(a) deg(b)ba
(since the wedge product of differential forms has these properties).
Moreover, if f : M →N is a differentiable map of manifolds then
we have the pullback map f ∗: Ωi(N) →Ωi(M) which commutes
with d and hence descends to the cohomology.
Also f ∗preserves
the wedge product, hence defines a graded algebra homomorphism
f ∗: H•(N, C) →H•(M, C).

Exercise 44.1. Let f : [0, 1] × M →N be a differentiable map and
ft : M →N be given by ft(x) = f(t, x). Then f ∗
0 = f ∗
1 on H•(N, C).
In other words, f ∗is invariant under (smooth) homotopies of f.

Lemma 44.2. (Cartan magic formula) Let v be a vector field on M,
Lv : Ωi(M) →Ωi(M) the Lie derivative and ιv : Ωi(M) →Ωi−1(M)
the contraction operator. Then

Lv = ιvd + dιv.

Proof. It suffices to check this identity on local charts. It is easy to see
that both sides are derivations, so it suffices to check the equation on
functions (0-forms) and on 1-forms of the form df where f is a function.
For functions we have Lvf = ιvdf, which is essentially the definition of
Lv, while for ω = df we have

Lv(df) = d(Lvf) = dιv(df) = (ιvd + dιv)(df),
202


since d2 = 0.
□

Corollary 44.3. Lv maps closed forms to exact forms, hence acts triv-
ially in cohomology.

Corollary 44.4. If a connected Lie group G acts on a manifold M
then G acts trivially on H•(M, C).

Theorem 44.5. Suppose a compact connected Lie group G acts on
a manifold M.
Then the cohomology H•(M, C) is computed by the
complex of invariant differential forms Ω•(M)G.

Proof. We have the averaging operator P : Ω•(M) →Ω•(M) over G
which commutes with d, so we have a decomposition

Ω•(M) = Ω•(M)G ⊕Ω•(M)0
where the first summand is the image of P and the second one is the
kernel of P. Now, if ω ∈Ωi(M)0 is closed than the cohomology class
[ω] of ω coincides with the cohomology class of [gω] for all g ∈G. Thus

[ω] =
�

G
[gω]dg =
��

G
gωdg
�
= 0.

Thus ω = dη for some η ∈Ωi(M). Then ω = (1 −P)ω = d(1 −P)η,
and (1−P)η ∈Ωi(M)0. So the complex Ω•(M)0 is exact, which implies
the statement.
□

Corollary 44.6. If G is a compact Lie group then H•(G, C) is com-
puted by the complex Ω•(G)G of left-invariant differential forms on G.

The complex Ω•(G)G is called the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex
of G, and it can be described purely algebraically. To this end, we will
need another lemma from basic differential geometry.

Now observe that Ωm(G)G = ∧mg∗. Thus we get

Corollary 44.9. For any Lie group G the complex Ω•(G)G coincides
with the complex

0 →C →g∗→(∧2g)∗→...(∧mg)∗→...

with differential defined by (9), where g = Lie(G)C.

This purely algebraic complex can be defined for a Lie algebra g over
any field (the equality d2 = 0 follows from the Jacobi identity).11 It is
called the standard complex or the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex
of g, denoted CE•(g), and its cohomology is called the Lie algebra
cohomology of g, denoted H•(g).
Also note that the complex CE•(g) has wedge product multiplica-
tion, which descends to the cohomology. Thus H•(g) is a graded alge-
bra. Also, if g = Lie(G)C for a compact connected Lie group G then
H•(g) ∼= H•(G, C) as a graded algebra. However, this may fail even at
the level of vector spaces (i.e., Betti numbers) if G is not compact.

Example 44.10. Let g be abelian, dim g < ∞.
Then CE•(g) =
∧•g∗, with zero differential, so H•(g) = (∧•g)∗. So if G = (S1)n is a
torus then we get H•(G, C) = ∧•(ξ1, ..., ξn) where ξi have degree 1. It
particular, H•(S1) = ∧•(ξ). However, for the universal cover R of S1

this is clearly false.

Remark 44.11. Corollary 44.9 implies that for compact Lie groups
G, K the map Ω•(G) ⊗Ω•(K) →Ω•(G × K) (i.e., in components,
Ωi(G)⊗Ωj(K) →Ωi+j(G×K)) defines an isomorphism of cohomology
rings H•(G, C)⊗H•(K, C) →H•(G×K). This is a special case of the
K¨unneth theorem, which actually holds for any manifolds (and more
generally for sufficiently nice topological spaces), which need not have
any group structure. We warn the reader, however, that the tensor
product of algebras here is in the graded sense, i.e.

(a ⊗b)(a′ ⊗b′) = (−1)deg(b) deg(a′)(aa′ ⊗bb′).

11Note that if g is finite dimensional then ∧ig∗= (∧ig)∗.
204


Theorem 44.12. If G is a connected compact Lie group with Lie(G)C =
g then H•(G, C) ∼= (∧•g∗)g as a ring.

Proof. We have an action of G × G on G, so the cohomology of G is
computed by the complex of invariants Ω•(G)G×G = (∧•g∗)G. So our
job is to show that the differential in this complex is actually zero.
But this follows immediately from the definition of the differential in
∧•g∗.
□

Corollary 44.13. If G is a compact connected Lie group and Γ ⊂
G a finite subgroup then π∗defines an isomorphism H•(G/Γ, C) →
H•(G, C).

Thus it suffices to determine the cohomology of simple, simply con-
nected compact Lie groups.
To understand the algebra R := H•(G) = H•(G, C) better, note
that the multiplication map G × G →G induces the graded algebra
homomorphism ∆: H•(G) →H•(G × G) = H•(G) ⊗H•(G), which
is coassociative: (∆⊗id)∆= (id ⊗∆)∆.
Such a map is called a
coproduct since it defines an algebra structure on R∗. We also have
the augmentation map ε : R →C such that

(ε ⊗1)(∆(x)) = (1 ⊗ε)(∆(x)) = x

for all x ∈R. Such a structure is called a graded bialgebra. 12

Exercise 44.14. (Hopf theorem) Use the graded bialgebra structure
on R to show that R is a free graded commutative algebra on some
homogeneous generators. Deduce that all generators must be of odd
degree, so R = ∧•(ξ1, ..., ξr) with deg ξi = 2mi+1 for some nonnegative

45. Lecture 20

45.1. Cohomology of homogeneous spaces. Let G be a compact
Lie group, g = Lie(G)C and K ⊂G a closed subgroup, k = Lie(K)C,
and consider the homogeneous space G/K. How to compute the coho-
mology H•(G/K)?
Since the group G acts on G/K, this cohomology is computed by
the complex Ω•(G/K)G = (∧•(g/k)∗)K. Let us denote this complex by
CE•(g, K). It is called the relative Chevalley-Eilenberg complex.
For example, if K = Γ is finite, this is just the Γ-invariant part of
the usual Chevalley-Eilenberg complex.
But Γ acts trivially on the
cohomology, so we get H•(G/Γ) = H•(G) (as already noted above).
But what happens if dim K > 0? Can we describe the differential in
this complex algebraically as we did for K = 1?
This is answered by the following proposition. Let k ⊂g be a pair of
Lie algebras (over any field, not necessarily finite dimensional). Denote
by CEi(g, k) the spaces ((∧•(g/k))∗)k.

Proposition 45.1. CE•(g, k) is a subcomplex of CE•(g).

Exercise 45.2. Prove Proposition 45.1.

Definition 45.3. The complex CE•(g, k) is called the relative Chevalley-
Eilenberg complex, and its cohomology is called the relative Lie
algebra cohomology, denoted by H•(g, k).

Now note that, going back to the setting of compact Lie groups, we
have CE•(g, K) = CE•(g, k)K/K◦, so we have

Corollary 45.4. H•(G/K) ∼= H•(g, k)K/K◦as algebras.

Thus, the computation of the cohomology of G/K reduces to the
computation of the relative Lie algebra cohomology, which is a again
purely algebraic problem.

Corollary 45.5. Suppose z ∈K is an element that acts by −1 on g/k.
Then (∧i(g/k)∗)K = 0 for odd i. Hence the differential in CE•(g, K)
is 0 and thus H•(G/K) ∼= (∧•(g/k)∗)K, with cohomology present only
in even degrees.

Now we can rederive the same formula for the Poincar´e polynomial
of the Grassmannian from the following fact from algebraic topology:

Proposition 46.5. If X is a connected cell complex which only has
even-dimensional cells, then the cohomology of X vanishes in odd de-
grees, and the groups H2i(X, Z) are free abelian groups of ranks b2i(X),
where the Betti number b2i(X) is just the number of cells in X of di-
mension i. Moreover, X is simply connected.

So we obtain an even stronger statement than before:

In particular, this gives Betti numbers over any field (including pos-

46.3. Flag manifolds. The flag manifold Fn(C) is the space of all
complete flags 0 = V0 ⊂V1 ⊂... ⊂Vn = Cn, where dim Vi = i.
Note that the flag manifold is a homogeneous space: Fn = G/T, where
G = U(n) and T = U(1)n is a maximal torus in G.
We have fibrations π : Fn(C) →CPn−1 sending (V1, ..., Vn−1) to
Vn−1, whose fiber is the space of flags in Vn−1, i.e., Fn−1(C).
This
shows, by induction, that flag manifolds can be decomposed into even-
dimensional cells isomorphic to Cr (also called Schubert cells). Thus
the Betti numbers of Fn vanish in odd degrees, and in even degrees are
given by the generating function
�
b2i(Fn)qn = [n]q! = (1 + q)(1 + q + q2)...(1 + q + ... + qn−1).

Remark 46.7. We have a map πm : Fm+n(C) →Gm+n,m(C) sending
(V1, ..., Vm+n−1) to Vm. This is a fibration with fiber Fm(C) × Fn(C).
Thus we get another proof of the formula for Betti numbers of the
Grassmannian.

We can also define the partial flag manifold FS(C), where S ⊂
[1, n −1] is a subset, namely the space of partial flags (Vs, s ∈S),
Vs ⊂Cn, dim Vs = s, Vs ⊂Vt if s < t.

47. Lecture 22

47.1. Cohomology of Lie algebras with coefficients. The defini-
tion of cohomology of Lie algebras may be generalized to define the
cohomology with coefficients in a module, so that the cohomology con-
sidered above is the one for the trivial module.
211


Let g be a Lie algebra and V a g-module. The Chevalley-Eilenberg
(or standard) complex of g with coefficients in V is the defined
by
CE•(g, V ) := Hom(∧•g, V )
with differential defined by the full Cartan formula (without dropping
the first term):

dω(a0, ..., am) =
�

i
(−1)iaiω(a0, ...,�ai, ..., am)+

�

i<j
(−1)i+jω([ai, aj], a0, ...,�ai, ..., �aj, ..., am).

The cohomology of this complex is called the cohomology of g with
coefficients in V and denoted H•(g, V ). Note that the previously
defined cohomology H•(g) is H•(g, C).
If g is the Lie algebra of a Lie group G (or its complexification) and V
is finite dimensional, then we simply have CE•(g, V ) := (Ω•(G) ⊗V )g

(and the differential is just the de Rham differential). So in particular
we have

Proposition 47.1. (i) If G is compact and V is a nontrivial irreducible
representation then
Hi(g, V ) = 0, i > 0.
In particular, this is so for any non-trivial irreducible finite dimensional
reporesentation V of a semisimple Lie algebra g.
(ii) (Whitehead’s lemma) For semisimple g and any finite dimen-
sional V we have H1(g, V ) = H2(g, V ) = 0.

However, this cohomology is non-trivial in general if g is not semisim-
ple or V is infinite dimensional.
Let us explore the meaning of Hi(g, V ) for small i.
1. We have H0(g, V ) = V g, the g-invariants in V .
2.
H1(g, V ) is the quotient of the space Z1(g, V ) of 1-cocycles
ω : g →V , i.e., linear maps satisfying

ω([x, y]) = xω(y) −yω(x)

by the space of 1-coboundaries B1(g, V ), of the form ω(x) = xv for
some v ∈V .

Proposition 47.2. (i) If V, W are representations of g then Ext1(V, W) =
H1(g, Homk(V, W)).
(ii) Consider the action of the additive group of V on the Lie algebra
g ⋉V (with trivial commutator on V ) by

v ◦(x, w) = (x, w + xv).
212

Proof. (i) Suppose the space W ⊕V is equipped with the action of g
so that W is a submodule and V the quotient. Thus the action of g on

i.e., ρ ∈Z1(g, Homk(V, W)). Also it is easy to check that for two such

3.
Z1(g, g) is the Lie algebra of derivations of g, and B1(g, g) is
the ideal of inner derivation. So H1(g, g) is the Lie algebra of outer
derivations, the quotient of all derivations by inner derivations.
4. Suppose we want to define an abelian extension �g of g by V ,
i.e., a Lie algebra which can be included in the short exact sequence

0 →V →�g →g →0

where V is an abelian ideal. To classify such extension, pick a vector
space splitting �g = g ⊕V , then the commutator looks like

[(x, v), (y, w)] = ([x, y], xw −yv + ω(x, y)),

where ω : ∧g →V is a linear map. The Jacobi identity is then equiva-
lent to ω being in the space Z2(g, V ) of 2-cocycles. Moreover, it is easy
to check that for two such extensions �g1,�g2 there is an isomorphism
φ : �g1 →�g2 which acts trivially on V and g if and only if the corre-
sponding cocycles ω1, ω2 differ by a coboundary: ω1 −ω2 ∈B2(g, V ).
Thus, we get

Proposition 47.3. Abelian extensions of g by V modulo isomorphisms
which act trivially on g and V are classified by H2(g, V ). For example,
the space H2(g, C) classifies 1-dimensional central extensions of g:

0 →C →�g →g →0.
213


Example 47.4. Let g = C2 be the 2-dimensional abelian Lie algebra.
Then we have seen that the Poincar´e polynomial of the cohomology of
g is 1 + 2q + q2 (cohomology of the 2-torus). So H2(g, C) = C. The
only cocycle up to scaling is given by ω(x, y) = 1, where x, y is a basis
of g, and all coboundaries are zero. So we have a central extension of g
defined by this cocycle with basis x, y, c and [x, y] = c, [x, c] = [y, c] = 0.
This is the Heisenberg Lie algebra, which is isomorphic to the Lie
algebra of strictly upper-triangular 3 by 3 matrices.

5.
Let us now study deformations of Lie algebras.
Suppose g is
a Lie algebra and we want to deform the bracket, with deformation
parameter t. So the new bracket will be

[x, y]t = [x, y] + tc1(x, y) + t2c2(x, y) + ...,

where ci : ∧2g →g are linear maps. This bracket should satisfy the
Jacobi identity, i.e., define a new Lie algebra structure on g[[t]]. Such
deformations are distinguished up to linear isomorphisms

a = 1 + ta1 + t2a2 + ...

where ai ∈Endk(g).
In particular, in first order, i.e., modulo t2, we get a new Lie algebra
structure on g[t]/t2g[t] = g ⊕tg such that this Lie algebra can be
included in the short exact sequence

0 →tg →g ⊕tg →g →0

where tg is an abelian ideal. So this is an abelian extension of g by
tg, and we know that such extensions are classified by H2(g, g). So we
conclude that we have

Proposition 47.5. First-order deformations of g as a Lie algebra are
classified by H2(g, g).

Thus if H2(g, g) = 0, every deformation is isomorphic to the trivial
one, with c1 = c2 = ... = 0. Indeed, applying automorphisms a =
1 + ta1 + t2a2 + ..., we can kill successively c1, then c2, then c3, and so
on. Thus we have

Corollary 47.6. If g is semisimple then it is rigid, i.e., has no non-
trivial Lie algebra deformations.

Example 47.7. Let g be the 2-dimensional abelian Lie algebra. Then
H2(g, g) = C2, and we get a 2-parameter family of deformations with
bracket [x, y] = tx + sy. These, however, turn out to be all equivalent
(for (t, s) ̸= (0, 0)) under the action of GL2(C): they are all isomorphic
to the Lie algebra with basis x, y and commutator [x, y] = y.
214


However, not all first order deformations of a Lie algebra lift to
second order, i.e., modulo t3. Namely, The Jacobi identity in the second
order tells us that dc2 = [c1, c1], where [c1, c1] is the Schouten bracket
of c1 with itself, a certain quadratic expression in c1. This expression
is automatically a cocycle, but we need it to be a coboundary.
So
the cohomology class of [c1, c1] in H3(g, g) is an obstruction to lifting
the deformation modulo t3. Thus the space H3(g, g) is the home for
obstructions to deformations.
6. In a similar way we can study deformations V [[t]] of a module V
over g:
ρt(x) = ρ(x) + tρ1(x) + t2ρ2(x) + ...
Modulo t2 we get a g-module structure on V [t]/t2V [t] = V ⊕tV such
that we have a short exact sequence

0 →tV →V ⊕tV →V →0.

Thus first order deformations of V are classified by Ext1
g(V, V ) =
H1(g, EndkV ). Again, lifting of this deformation modulo t3 is not au-
tomatic, and we get an obstruction in Ext2
g(V, V ) = H2(g, Endk(V )).

47.2. Levi decomposition.

Theorem 47.8. (Levi decomposition) Over real or complex numbers
we have g ∼= rad(g)⊕gss, where gss ⊂g is a semisimple subalgebra (but
not necessarily an ideal); i.e., g is isomorphic to the semidirect product
gss ⋉rad(g). In other words, the projection p : g →gss admits an (in
general, non-unique) splitting q : gss →g, i.e., a Lie algebra map such
that p ◦q = Id.

Proof. We can write g = gss ⊕rad(g) as a vector space. Then the
commutator looks like

[(a, x), (b, y)] = ([x, b]−[y, a]+[a, b]+ω(x, y), [x, y]) x, y ∈gss, a, b ∈rad(g).

Let rad(g) = D0 ⊃D1 ⊃... be the upper central series of rad(g),
i.e., Di+1 = [Di, Di]. Suppose Dn ̸= 0 but Dn+1 = 0 (so Dn is an
abelian ideal). Using induction in dimension of g and replacing g by
g/Dn, we may assume that ω(x, y) ∈Dn. But then ω ∈Z2(gss, Dn),
which equals B2(gss, Dn) by Whitehead’s lemma, i.e., ω = dη. Using η,
we can modify the splitting g = gss ⊕rad(g) to make sure that ω = 0.
This implies the statement.
□

47.3. The nilradical. Consider now a solvable Lie algebra a and its
adjoint representation. By Lie’s theorem, in some basis a acts in this
representation by upper triangular matrices. Let n ⊂a be the subset of
nilpotent elements (the nilradical of a). Thus n is the set of x ∈a that
215


act in this basis by strictly upper triangular matrices. In particular,
n ⊃[a, a], so a/n is abelian. Moreover each diagonal entry of these
upper triangular matrices gives rise to a character λ ∈(a/n)∗.
By
definition of n, these characters form a spanning set in (a/n)∗.

Proposition 47.9. If d : a →a is a derivation then d(a) ⊂n.

Proof. Since there are finitely many characters λ, for each of them we
have etdλ = λ. It follows that d acts on a/n trivially.
□

Thus if a = rad(g) is the radical of g then g acts trivially on a/n.

47.4. Exponentiating nilpotent and solvable Lie algebras and
the third Lie theorem. Let g be a finite dimensional solvable Lie
algebra over K = R or C.

Theorem 47.10. There is a simply connected Lie group G over K
with Lie(G) = g, such that the exponential map exp : g →G is a
diffeomorphism. Moreover, if g is nilpotent and if we identify G with
g under this diffeomorphism then the multiplication map g × g →g is
polynomial.

Example 47.11. Let g be the Heisenberg Lie algebra, i.e. the Lie
algebra of strictly upper triangular matrices. Then the multiplication
map has the form

(x, y, z) ∗(x′, y′, z′) = (x + x′, y + y′, z + z′ + 1

2(xy′ −x′y)).

Proof. The proof is by induction in dim g. Namely, fix a nonzero ho-
momorphism χ : g →K (which exists since g is solvable), and let
g0 = Kerχ. Then we have g = Kd ⋉g0, the semidirect product, where
d ∈g acts as a derivation on g0. Let G0 be the simply connected Lie
group corresponding to g0, which is defined by the induction assump-
tion, and exp : g0 →G0 is a diffeomorphism. So we have a regular
multiplication map P : g0 ×g0 →g0, and we have a 1-parameter group
of automorphisms etd : g0 →g0. Thus we can define a group structure
on g by the formula

(x, t) ∗(y, s) = (P(x, etd(y)), t + s), x, y ∈g0, t, s ∈K.

It is not difficult to check that the exponential map for this group is a
diffeomorphism, and the multiplication written using this identification
is given by a polynomial if g is nilpotent. Namely,

exp(td + x) = (td, xt),

where

xt =

□

Definition 47.12. The simply connected Lie group whose Lie algebra
is nilpotent is called unipotent.13

Corollary 47.13. (Third Lie theorem) For any finite dimensional Lie
algebra g over R or C there is a simply connected Lie group G with
Lie(G) = g.

Proof. By the previous theorem, we have such a group A for a = rad(g).
Moreover, by the Levi decomposition, the simply connected semisimple
group Gss corresponding to gss acts on rad(g). Hence by the second Lie
theorem, Gss acts on A, and the simply connected Lie group Gss ⋉A
has the Lie algebra gss ⋉rad(g) = g.
□

Corollary 47.14. A simply connected complex Lie group G is of the
form Gss⋉A, where A is solvable simply connected, hence diffeomorphic
to Cn. Thus G has the homotopy type of Gc
ss.

48.1. Algebraic Lie algebras. Let us say that a finite dimensional
complex Lie algebra g is algebraic if g is the Lie algebra of a group
G = K ⋉N, where K is a reductive group and N a unipotent group.

Example 48.1. Let g1 be a 3-dimensional Lie algebra with basis d, x, y
and [x, y] = 0, [d, x] = x, [d, y] =
√

2y. Similarly, let g2 have basis
d, x, y with [x, y] = 0, [d, x] = x, [d, y] = y + x. Then g1, g2 are not
algebraic (check it!).

Proposition 48.2. Any finite dimensional complex Lie algebra is a
Lie subalgebra of an algebraic one.

Proof. Let us say that g is n-algebraic if it is the Lie algebra of a group
G := K ⋉A, where K is reductive and a = Lie(A) is solvable with
dim(a/n) ≤n, where n is the nilradical of a. Thus 0-algebraic is the
same as algebraic. Note that for any g we have the Levi decomposition
g = gss ⋉a, where a = rad(g), which shows that any g is n-algebraic
for some n. So it suffices to show that any n-algebraic Lie algebra for
n > 0 embeds into an n −1-algebraic one.
To this end, let g = Lie(G) be n-algebraic, with G = K ⋉A and A
simply connected. Let a = Lie(A), so dim(a/n) = n. Pick d ∈a, d /∈n
such that d is K-invariant. This can be done since K acts trivially
on a/n and its representations are completely reducible. We have a

48.2. Faithful representations of nilpotent Lie algebras. Let n
be a finite dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra over C. In this subsection
we will show that n has a finite dimensional faithful representation.
To this end, recall that n = Lie(N) where N is a simply connected
Lie group, and the exponential map exp : n →N is bijective. Moreover,
the multiplication law of N, when rewritten on n using the exponential
map, is given by polynomials.

Proposition 48.4. Let O(N) be the space of polynomial functions on
N ∼= n (identified using the exponential map). Then O(N) is invariant
under the action of n by left-invariant vector fields. Moreover, we have
a canonical filtration O(N) = ∪n≥1Vn, where Vn ⊂O(N) are finite
dimensional subspaces such that V1 ⊂V2 ⊂... and nVn ⊂Vn−1.

Proof. Let P : n × n →n be the polynomial multiplication law. Let
x ∈n and Lx the corresponding left-invariant vector field. Let f ∈
218


O(N) = Sn∗. Then for y ∈n we have

(Lxf)(y) = d

dt|t=0f(P(y, tx)).

Since f and P are polynomials, this is clearly a polynomial in y. Thus
Lx : O(N) →O(N).
We have a lower central series filtration on n:

n = D0(n) ⊃[n, n] = D1(n) ⊃... ⊃Dm(n) = 0.

This gives an ascending filtration

0 = D0(n)⊥⊂.... ⊂Dm(n)⊥= n∗.

We assign to Dj(n)⊥filtration degree dj, where d is a sufficiently large
positive integer. This gives rise to an ascending filtration F • on Sn∗=
O(N). Note that

P(x, y) = x + y +
�

i≥1
Qi(x, y),

where Qi : n × n →[n, n] has degree i in x. Thus

(Lxf)(y) = (∂xf)(y) + (∂Q1(x,y)f)(y).

The first term clearly lowers the degree, and so does the second one if
d is large enough. So we may take Vn = Fn(Sn∗) to be the space of
polynomials of degree ≤n, then LxVn ⊂Vn−1, as claimed.
□

Example 48.5. We illustrate this proof on the example of the Heisen-
berg algebra H = ⟨x, y, c⟩with [x, y] = c and [x, c] = [y, c] = 0. In this
case
etxesy = etx+sy+ 1

Thus

We have D1(H) = Cc, so D1(H)⊥is spanned by p, q. Thus we have
deg(p) = deg(q) = d, deg(r) = d2. So for any d > 1, Lc, Lx, Ly lower
the degree. So setting Vn = Fn(SH∗) to be the (finite dimensional)
space of polynomials of degree ≤n, we see that Lc, Lx, Ly map Vn to
Vn−1.

Corollary 48.6. Every finite dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra n over
C has a faithful finite dimensional representation where all its elements
act by nilpotent operators. Thus n is isomorphic to a subalgebra of the
Lie algebra of strictly upper triangular matrices of some size.
219


Proof. By definition, O(N) is a faithful n-module. Hence so is Vn for
some n.
□

48.3. Faithful representations of general finite dimensional Lie
algebras.

Theorem 48.7. (Ado’s theorem) Every finite dimensional Lie algebra
over C has a finite dimensional faithful representation.

Proof. Let g be a finite dimensional complex Lie algebra. Since g can
be embedded into an algebraic Lie algebra, we may assume without loss
of generality that g is algebraic. Thus g = Lie(G) where G = K⋉N for
reductive K and unipotent N. Also we may assume that g ̸= g′ ⊕g′′

for g′, g′′ ̸= 0, otherwise the problem reduces to a smaller algebraic
Lie algebra (indeed if V ′, V ′′ are faithful representations of g′, g′′ then
V ′ ⊕V ′′ is a faithful representation of g′ ⊕g′′).
Then k = Lie(K)
acts faithfully on n = Lie(N). Now, g acts on O(N) preserving the
subspaces Vn (n = Lie(N) acts by left invariant vector fields and k by
adjoint action).
As we have shown, n acts faithfully on Vn for some n. We claim
that this Vn is, in fact, a faithful representation of the whole g, which
implies the theorem. Indeed, let a ⊂g be the ideal of elements acting
by zero on Vn, and let a be the projection of a to k (an ideal in k).
Since n acts faithfully on Vn, we have a ∩n = 0. Given a ∈a, we have
a = a + b where a ∈a is the projection of a and b ∈n. For x ∈n
we have [a, x] ∈a ∩n = 0. Thus [a, x] = −[b, x]. Hence the operator
x �→[a, x] on n is nilpotent. So a acts on n by nilpotent operators.
Since K is reductive and a ⊂k is an ideal, this means that a acts on n
by zero. Thus a = 0 and a ⊂n. Hence a = 0.
□

49. Lecture 24

49.1. Borel subgroups and subalgebras. Let G be a connected
complex reductive Lie group, g = Lie(G). Fix a Cartan subalegbra
h ⊂g with a system of positive roots Π, and consider the corresponding
triangular decomposition g = n−⊕h ⊕n+, where n+ is spanned by
positive root elements and n−by negative root elements. Let H be the
maximal torus in G corresponding to h, N+ the unipotent subgroup of
G corresponding to n+, and B+ = HN+ the solvable subgroup with
Lie(B+) = b+ := h ⊕n+; these are all closed Lie subgroups.

Definition 49.1. A Borel subalgebra of g is a Lie subalgebra con-
jugate to b+. A Borel subgroup of G is a Lie subgroup conjugate to
B+. A parabolic subalgebra (subgroup) is a Lie subalgebra of g
(subgroup of G) containing a Borel subalgebra (subgroup).
220


Since all pairs (h, Π) are conjugate, this definition does not depend
on the choice of (h, Π).

Lemma 49.2. B+ is its own normalizer in G.

Proof. Let γ ∈G be such that Adγ(B+) = B+. Let H′ = Adγ(H) ⊂
B+. It is easy to show that we can conjugate H′ back into H inside
B+, so we may assume without loss of generality that H′ = H. Then
γ ∈N(H), and it preserves positive roots. Hence the image of γ in W
is 1, so γ ∈H, as claimed.
□

49.2. Flag manifold. Thus the set of all Borel subalgebras (or sub-
groups) in G is the homogeneous space G/B+, a complex manifold. It
is called the flag manifold of G. Note that it only depends on the
semisimple part gss ⊂g.
Let Gc ⊂G be the compact form of G, with Lie algebra gc ⊂g. It
is easy to see that gc + b+ = g. Thus the Gc-orbit Gc · 1 of 1 ∈G/B+
contains a neighborhood of 1 in G/B+. Hence the same holds for any
point of this orbit, i.e., Gc · 1 ⊂G/B+ is a submanifold of the same
dimension, i.e., an open subset. But it is also compact, since Gc is
compact, hence closed. As G/B+ is connected, we get that Gc · 1 =
G/B+, i.e., Gc acts transitively on G/B+.
Also the Cartan involution maps positive roots to negative ones, so
Gc ∩B+ ⊂w0(B+) ∩B+ = H. Thus Gc ∩B+ = Hc, a maximal torus
in Gc. So we get

Proposition 49.3. We have G/B+ = Gc/Hc. In particular, G/B+ is
a compact complex manifold of dimension |R+| = 1

2(dim g −rankg).

Let A = exp(ihc) ⊂H, K = Gc, N = N+.

Corollary 49.4. (The Iwasawa decomposition of G) The multipli-
cation map K × A × N →G is a diffeomorphism. In particular, we
have G = KAN.

Example 49.5. 1. For G = SL2 we have G/B+ = SU(2)/U(1) = S2,
the Riemann sphere.
2. For G = GLn we have G/B+ = Fn, the set of flags in Cn that we
considered before.

Another realization of the flag manifold is one as the G-orbit of
the line spanned by the highest weight vector in an irreducible rep-
resentation with a regular highest weight. Namely, let λ ∈P+ be a
dominant integral weight with λ(hi) ≥1 for all i (so the smallest such
λ is λ = ρ). Let Lλ be the corresponding irreducible representation.
We have b+ · Cvλ = Cvλ, but eαvλ ̸= 0 for any α ∈R−, so b+ is the
221


stabilizer of Cvλ in g. Thus any g ∈G which preserves Cvλ belongs
to the normalizer of b+ (or, equivalently, B+), i.e., g ∈B+.
Thus
O := G · Cvλ ⊂PLλ is identified with G/B+. This shows that O is
compact, hence closed, i.e., O = G/B+ is a complex projective variety.

49.3. Borel fixed point theorem.

Theorem 49.6. Let a be a solvable Lie algebra over C, V a finite
dimensional a-module. Let X ⊂PV be a closed a-invariant subset.
Then there exists x ∈X fixed by a.

Proof. The proof is by induction in n = dim a. The base n = 0 is trivial,
so we only need to justify the induction step. Since a is solvable, it has
an ideal a′ of codimension 1. By the induction assumption, Y := Xa′

is a nonempty closed subset of X, so it suffices to show that the 1-
dimensional Lie algebra a/a′ has a fixed point on Y . Thus it suffices
to prove the theorem for n = 1.
So let a be 1-dimensional, spanned by a ∈a. We can choose the
normalization of a so that all eigenvalues of a on V have different real
parts. Fix x0 ∈X and consider the curve etax0 for t ∈R. It is easy
to see that there exists x := limt→∞etax0. Then x is fixed by a, as
desired.
□

49.4. Maximal solvable subalgebras. Note that b+ is a maximal
solvable subalgebra of g; any bigger (parabolic) subalgebra will contain
some negative root vector, hence the corresponding sl2-subalgebra, so
it will not be solvable. Moreover, B+ is a maximal solvable subgroup
of G: if P ⊃B+ then some element g ∈P does not normalize b+, so
Lie(P) has to be larger than b+, hence not solvable. Thus any Borel
subalgebra (subgroup) is a maximal solvable one. It turns out that the
converse also holds.

Proposition 49.7. Any solvable subalgebra of g (connected solvable
subgroup of G) is contained in a Borel subalgebra (subgroup).

Proof. Let a ⊂g be a solvable subalgebra. By the Borel fixed point
theorem, a has a fixed point b ∈G/B+. Thus a normalizes b. Hence
a ⊂b, as claimed.
□

Corollary 49.8. Any element of g is contained in a Borel subalgebra
b ⊂g.

Let us say that a Lie subalgebra a ⊂g is a nilpotent subalgebra if
it consists of nilpotent elements. Note that this is a stronger condition
than just being nilpotent as a Lie algebra.
222


Corollary 49.9. Any nilpotent subalgebra of g is conjugate to a Lie
subalgebra of n+. Thus n+ is a maximal nilpotent subalgebra of g, and
any maximal nilpotent subalgebra of g is conjugate to n+.

Proof. There is g ∈G such that Ad(g)a ⊂b+, but since a is nilpotent
we actually have Ad(g)a ⊂n+.
□

A similar result holds for groups, with the same proof:

Corollary 49.10. Any unipotent subgroup of G is conjugate to a (closed)
Lie subgroup of N+. Thus N+ is a maximal unipotent subgroup of G,
and any maximal unipotent subgroup of G is conjugate to N+.

We also have

Proposition 49.11. The normalizer of n+ and N+ in G is B+. Thus
every maximal nilpotent subalgebra (unipotent subgroup) is contained in
a unique Borel. Hence such subalgebras (subgroups) are parametrized
by the flag manifold G/B+.

Proof. Clearly B+ is contained in the normalizer of N+, so this normal-
izer is a parabolic subgroup. We have seen that such subgroup, if larger
than B+, must have a larger Lie algebra that b+, so it contains some
root sl2-subalgebra. But the group corresponding to such subalgebra
does not normalize n+, a contradiction.
□

223
